Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.

I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.

People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. Just the cabinet door.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.

We had this whole display set up near the front doors, with the fake grass and everything, and this one lady came in and asked if the umbrellas were waterproof. I mean, they're umbrellas, right? But she just kept poking at the fabric. She had this bright yellow coat on, like a raincoat, even though it wasn't raining. It was actually really sunny that day, unseasonably warm. People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No head injuries. I mean, I did hit my head on the kitchen cabinet a couple of weeks ago. I was trying to reach the top shelf where we keep the good coffee mugs. My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. The price of gas just went up another ten cents yesterday. I saw it on the sign when I was driving to work. But yeah, no strokes or seizures or anything wrong with my brain like that. Just the cabinet door.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever stutter or have similar speech problems as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 59 / first_line_supervisor_of_retail_sales_worker / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\nmanifestation: \"The jumbled explanations and odd stops on the sales floor have been noticeable for about six months now.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, definitely not since I was a kid. It's really just been the last six months or so. It's been pretty noticeable at work, actually, out on the sales floor. I'll be trying to explain a task to one of the associates and my explanations just get all jumbled up, or I'll do these odd stops right in the middle of a sentence. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I mean, I try to explain the inventory numbers to the team, or the weekend schedule, but my words just start jumping. Like, I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm thinking about Thursdays, and then the church grill. We had this big barbecue at church last summer, actually. It rained, but we still managed to cook the hotdogs. Anyway, my boss's tone just gets so sharp when I do this, and people just look at me like they can't follow what I'm saying. They just stare. I don't know why they can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to speak this way, or is this a new change for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. I'd say it's been about six months since I started getting so jumbled on the sales floor. The odd stops, the words getting tangled up... it's been about half a year. Which is weird because six months ago, that was right around when my sister bought her new car. A blue sedan. She got a great deal on it, too, negotiated the price down and everything. She always was good at haggling. We used to go to garage sales when we were kids, and she'd always get the best toys for pennies. I remember this one time she got a whole bucket of toy soldiers... but yeah, on the sales floor, it's just been the last six months or so that my words started doing this.\n\nDoctor: During this same six-month period, have you had times when you felt unusually energized or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energized? No, not really. I sleep about the same as I always do. I try to get a solid seven hours, though the streetlamp outside my bedroom window makes it tricky sometimes. The city put in these new LED bulbs last fall, and they are just incredibly bright. Almost blinding, really. It reminds me of the fluorescent lights we have over the clearance section at work. We had to replace the ballasts in those fixtures twice last year because they kept flickering. The maintenance guy, Frank, he said the wiring in the building is older than he is. He’s a good guy, Frank. Always brings donuts on Fridays. Glazed, usually, from that little bakery down on 4th street. They do a great apple fritter, too, the really crispy kind. But, um... no, I haven't had any bursts of energy or felt like I didn't need to sleep. I get my regular rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? I mean, not that I can think of. Sometimes the store gets really loud when the weekend rush hits, and it's hard to hear anything over the PA system. The speakers are right above the registers, and they just blare the promotional announcements on a loop. \"Save twenty percent on all patio furniture,\" over and over. Which is funny because nobody buys patio furniture in November. We had this whole display set up near the front doors, with the fake grass and everything, and this one lady came in and asked if the umbrellas were waterproof. I mean, they're umbrellas, right? But she just kept poking at the fabric. She had this bright yellow coat on, like a raincoat, even though it wasn't raining. It was actually really sunny that day, unseasonably warm. People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No head injuries. I mean, I did hit my head on the kitchen cabinet a couple of weeks ago. I was trying to reach the top shelf where we keep the good coffee mugs. My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. But she just kept poking at the fabric. She had this bright yellow coat on, like a raincoat, even though it wasn't raining. It was actually really sunny that day, unseasonably warm. People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No head injuries. I mean, I did hit my head on the kitchen cabinet a couple of weeks ago. I was trying to reach the top shelf where we keep the good coffee mugs. My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. The price of gas just went up another ten cents yesterday. I saw it on the sign when I was driving to work. But yeah, no strokes or seizures or anything wrong with my brain like that. Just the cabinet door.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever stutter or have similar speech problems as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not since I was a kid. It's really just been the last six months or so. It's been pretty noticeable at work, actually, out on the sales floor. I'll be trying to explain a task to one of the associates and my explanations just get all jumbled up, or I'll do these odd stops right in the middle of a sentence. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. Last year he put in this massive above-ground pool, but he didn't level the ground right, so the water is like three inches higher on one side. It drives me crazy just looking at it over the fence. No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.

It rained, but we still managed to cook the hotdogs. Anyway, my boss's tone just gets so sharp when I do this, and people just look at me like they can't follow what I'm saying. They just stare. I don't know why they can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to speak this way, or is this a new change for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. I'd say it's been about six months since I started getting so jumbled on the sales floor. The odd stops, the words getting tangled up... it's been about half a year. Which is weird because six months ago, that was right around when my sister bought her new car. A blue sedan. She got a great deal on it, too, negotiated the price down and everything. She always was good at haggling. We used to go to garage sales when we were kids, and she'd always get the best toys for pennies. I remember this one time she got a whole bucket of toy soldiers... but yeah, on the sales floor, it's just been the last six months or so that my words started doing this.\n\nDoctor: During this same six-month period, have you had times when you felt unusually energized or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energized? No, not really. I sleep about the same as I always do. I try to get a solid seven hours, though the streetlamp outside my bedroom window makes it tricky sometimes. The city put in these new LED bulbs last fall, and they are just incredibly bright. Almost blinding, really. It reminds me of the fluorescent lights we have over the clearance section at work. We had to replace the ballasts in those fixtures twice last year because they kept flickering. The maintenance guy, Frank, he said the wiring in the building is older than he is. He’s a good guy, Frank. Always brings donuts on Fridays. Glazed, usually, from that little bakery down on 4th street. They do a great apple fritter, too, the really crispy kind. But, um... no, I haven't had any bursts of energy or felt like I didn't need to sleep. I get my regular rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? I mean, not that I can think of. Sometimes the store gets really loud when the weekend rush hits, and it's hard to hear anything over the PA system. The speakers are right above the registers, and they just blare the promotional announcements on a loop. \"Save twenty percent on all patio furniture,\" over and over. Which is funny because nobody buys patio furniture in November. We had this whole display set up near the front doors, with the fake grass and everything, and this one lady came in and asked if the umbrellas were waterproof. I mean, they're umbrellas, right? But she just kept poking at the fabric. She had this bright yellow coat on, like a raincoat, even though it wasn't raining. It was actually really sunny that day, unseasonably warm. People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No head injuries. I mean, I did hit my head on the kitchen cabinet a couple of weeks ago. I was trying to reach the top shelf where we keep the good coffee mugs. My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. The price of gas just went up another ten cents yesterday. I saw it on the sign when I was driving to work. But yeah, no strokes or seizures or anything wrong with my brain like that. Just the cabinet door.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever stutter or have similar speech problems as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not since I was a kid. It's really just been the last six months or so. It's been pretty noticeable at work, actually, out on the sales floor. I'll be trying to explain a task to one of the associates and my explanations just get all jumbled up, or I'll do these odd stops right in the middle of a sentence. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. Last year he put in this massive above-ground pool, but he didn't level the ground right, so the water is like three inches higher on one side. It drives me crazy just looking at it over the fence. I tried to tell him he needed to lay down some sand first, but Gary never listens. He's a retired plumber, so he thinks he knows everything about water. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed any problems with your memory or thinking that are new around the same time as the speech changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates":["F80.81","F98.5","F06.8"],"note":"","is_final":false}

---------- turn 14 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gemini-3.1-pro-preview ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {

No, I never had any stuttering or blocks or anything like that when I was a kid. I always spoke pretty clearly, actually. I even used to do the morning announcements in middle school over the PA system. Speaking of PA systems, the intercom at the store has been acting up for weeks now. You press the button on the register phone to page someone to the paint desk, and all you get is this loud, screeching feedback sound. It just pierces right through your ears. One of our cashiers, Brenda, she accidentally left the page button locked the other day and the whole store had to listen to that awful static for like five minutes until I ran up to the front to fix it. Brenda's a sweet lady, but she is just not great with technology. She actually still uses a flip phone. I tried to explain to her that she could get a smartphone for pretty cheap these days, especially if you buy them refurbished. My son upgraded his a few months ago and gave me his old one, so I know you can get good use out of the older models. You just have to make sure you buy a heavy-duty case for it. I got one of those OtterBox ones. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.

We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I understand people just fine. I hear what they're saying and I get it. It's just my own mouth that's the issue. But listening? No problem. Like, I listen to the customers all day long. Some of them, though, they don't even know what they want when they come in. A guy came in yesterday looking for a specific type of anchor for drywall, but he kept calling it a "wall screw." I had to ask him like five questions just to figure out he needed a toggle bolt. Drywall is tricky, you know? If you don't hit a stud, whatever you hang is just going to rip right out of the gypsum. My brother-in-law tried to mount a 65-inch TV in his living room without finding the studs first. He just used regular plastic anchors. Middle of the night, crash! The whole thing came down, took a huge chunk of the wall with it, and completely smashed his glass coffee table. It was a nightmare to clean up. Glass was everywhere, even in the carpet fibers. You really need a good shop vac for something like that, not just a regular household vacuum. But anyway, yeah, I understand people perfectly fine.

They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not since I was a kid. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. Last year he put in this massive above-ground pool, but he didn't level the ground right, so the water is like three inches higher on one side. It drives me crazy just looking at it over the fence. I tried to tell him he needed to lay down some sand first, but Gary never listens. He's a retired plumber, so he thinks he knows everything about water. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed any problems with your memory or thinking that are new around the same time as the speech changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have trouble with stuttering or repeated sound blocks when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: No, I never had any stuttering or blocks or anything like that when I was a kid. I always spoke pretty clearly, actually. I even used to do the morning announcements in middle school over the PA system. Speaking of PA systems, the intercom at the store has been acting up for weeks now. You press the button on the register phone to page someone to the paint desk, and all you get is this loud, screeching feedback sound. It just pierces right through your ears. One of our cashiers, Brenda, she accidentally left the page button locked the other day and the whole store had to listen to that awful static for like five minutes until I ran up to the front to fix it. Brenda's a sweet lady, but she is just not great with technology. She actually still uses a flip phone. I tried to explain to her that she could get a smartphone for pretty cheap these days, especially if you buy them refurbished. My son upgraded his a few months ago and gave me his old one, so I know you can get good use out of the older models. You just have to make sure you buy a heavy-duty case for it. I got one of those OtterBox ones. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty understanding other people when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I understand people just fine.
