My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. The price of gas just went up another ten cents yesterday. I saw it on the sign when I was driving to work. But yeah, no strokes or seizures or anything wrong with my brain like that. Just the cabinet door.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever stutter or have similar speech problems as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not since I was a kid. It's really just been the last six months or so. It's been pretty noticeable at work, actually, out on the sales floor. I'll be trying to explain a task to one of the associates and my explanations just get all jumbled up, or I'll do these odd stops right in the middle of a sentence. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. Last year he put in this massive above-ground pool, but he didn't level the ground right, so the water is like three inches higher on one side. It drives me crazy just looking at it over the fence. I tried to tell him he needed to lay down some sand first, but Gary never listens. He's a retired plumber, so he thinks he knows everything about water. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed any problems with your memory or thinking that are new around the same time as the speech changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have trouble with stuttering or repeated sound blocks when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: No, I never had any stuttering or blocks or anything like that when I was a kid. I always spoke pretty clearly, actually. I even used to do the morning announcements in middle school over the PA system. Speaking of PA systems, the intercom at the store has been acting up for weeks now. You press the button on the register phone to page someone to the paint desk, and all you get is this loud, screeching feedback sound. It just pierces right through your ears. One of our cashiers, Brenda, she accidentally left the page button locked the other day and the whole store had to listen to that awful static for like five minutes until I ran up to the front to fix it. Brenda's a sweet lady, but she is just not great with technology. She actually still uses a flip phone. I tried to explain to her that she could get a smartphone for pretty cheap these days, especially if you buy them refurbished. My son upgraded his a few months ago and gave me his old one, so I know you can get good use out of the older models. You just have to make sure you buy a heavy-duty case for it. I got one of those OtterBox ones. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty understanding other people when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I understand people just fine. I hear what they're saying and I get it. It's just my own mouth that's the issue. But listening? No problem. Like, I listen to the customers all day long. Some of them, though, they don't even know what they want when they come in. A guy came in yesterday looking for a specific type of anchor for drywall, but he kept calling it a \"wall screw.\" I had to ask him like five questions just to figure out he needed a toggle bolt. Drywall is tricky, you know? If you don't hit a stud, whatever you hang is just going to rip right out of the gypsum. My brother-in-law tried to mount a 65-inch TV in his living room without finding the studs first. He just used regular plastic anchors. Middle of the night, crash! The whole thing came down, took a huge chunk of the wall with it, and completely smashed his glass coffee table. It was a nightmare to clean up. Glass was everywhere, even in the carpet fibers. You really need a good shop vac for something like that, not just a regular household vacuum. But anyway, yeah, I understand people perfectly fine.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it's definitely more jumbled. Not stuck sounds or anything like that. I actually came in because when I try to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to the floor staff, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the delivery trucks to Thursdays to the church grill to my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. It's frustrating because it makes sense in my head. 

But speaking of the church grill, we really need to replace the grates on that thing before the spring picnic. Cast iron is the only way to go, but you have to season it right or it's useless. You rub it down with oil and bake it. If you don't, it rusts so fast. My wife left my favorite cast iron skillet soaking in soapy water in the sink overnight once, and it was a mess. I spent an hour scrubbing it with coarse steel wool the next morning. We sell a ton of steel wool in the paint aisle, actually. Mostly to people refinishing old furniture. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. You really have to wear a good N95 mask, otherwise you'll be blowing brown dust out of your nose for a week.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I came in because... I mean, I try to explain the inventory numbers to the team, or the weekend schedule, but my words just start jumping. Like, I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm thinking about Thursdays, and then the church grill. We had this big barbecue at church last summer, actually. It rained, but we still managed to cook the hotdogs. Anyway, my boss's tone just gets so sharp when I do this, and people just look at me like they can't follow what I'm saying. They just stare. I don't know why they can't keep up.\n\nDoctor: Have you always tended to speak this way, or is this a new change for you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it hasn't always been like this. I'd say it's been about six months since I started getting so jumbled on the sales floor. The odd stops, the words getting tangled up... it's been about half a year. Which is weird because six months ago, that was right around when my sister bought her new car. A blue sedan. She got a great deal on it, too, negotiated the price down and everything. She always was good at haggling. We used to go to garage sales when we were kids, and she'd always get the best toys for pennies. I remember this one time she got a whole bucket of toy soldiers... but yeah, on the sales floor, it's just been the last six months or so that my words started doing this.\n\nDoctor: During this same six-month period, have you had times when you felt unusually energized or needed much less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Energized? No, not really. I sleep about the same as I always do. I try to get a solid seven hours, though the streetlamp outside my bedroom window makes it tricky sometimes. The city put in these new LED bulbs last fall, and they are just incredibly bright. Almost blinding, really. It reminds me of the fluorescent lights we have over the clearance section at work. We had to replace the ballasts in those fixtures twice last year because they kept flickering. The maintenance guy, Frank, he said the wiring in the building is older than he is. He’s a good guy, Frank. Always brings donuts on Fridays. Glazed, usually, from that little bakery down on 4th street. They do a great apple fritter, too, the really crispy kind. But, um... no, I haven't had any bursts of energy or felt like I didn't need to sleep. I get my regular rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that other people don’t seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Hearing or seeing things? I mean, not that I can think of. Sometimes the store gets really loud when the weekend rush hits, and it's hard to hear anything over the PA system. The speakers are right above the registers, and they just blare the promotional announcements on a loop. \"Save twenty percent on all patio furniture,\" over and over. Which is funny because nobody buys patio furniture in November. We had this whole display set up near the front doors, with the fake grass and everything, and this one lady came in and asked if the umbrellas were waterproof. I mean, they're umbrellas, right? But she just kept poking at the fabric. She had this bright yellow coat on, like a raincoat, even though it wasn't raining. It was actually really sunny that day, unseasonably warm. People were walking around in shorts. But yeah, no, I don't think I'm seeing anything that isn't there. Just the regular stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, did you have a hard time understanding social cues or making friends?\n\nPatient: Social cues? No, I don't think so. I had friends when I was little. We used to ride bikes down by the old creek behind my subdivision. We had these Schinn stingrays with the banana seats, you know? Mine was this metallic green color. I saved up my allowance for almost a year to buy it. Mowed a lot of lawns that summer. Mr. Henderson's lawn was the worst because he had this massive oak tree in the front yard, and the roots stuck up everywhere. You'd hit one with the mower blade and it would just jar your whole arm. He paid well, though. Five bucks a yard, which was a lot back then. You could buy a whole stack of comic books for five dollars. Superman, Batman, the classics. My mom threw all my comics out when I went off to college, which was a real shame because some of those first editions are probably worth a fortune now. I saw a guy on that antiques show once, he had a box of old comics and they appraised it for like, ten grand. Can you imagine? Just sitting in an attic. But, um, yeah, I made friends okay when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have very intense interests in specific topics that other people said were unusually focused or narrow?\n\nPatient: Intense interests? No, not really. I mean, I liked things, sure. Like the comics I mentioned. And baseball. I collected baseball cards for a while. Mostly just the local teams, but I had a few good ones. I kept them in this old cigar box my grandfather gave me. It smelled like cedar and stale tobacco, a really distinct smell. He used to smoke these long, dark cigars on the back porch after dinner. My grandmother hated it, always complaining about the smoke getting into the curtains. She had these heavy, velvet drapes in the living room, a deep burgundy color. They were supposed to keep the draft out during the winter, but that house was always freezing anyway. The furnace was this ancient, monstrous thing in the basement that clanked and groaned every time it kicked on. I was terrified of the basement when I was little. Thought there were monsters down there. But no, I wouldn't say I was unusually focused on anything. Just normal kid stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you’re talking, do you usually say very little and have trouble getting words out, or do you feel like you talk a normal amount but your speech becomes hard for others to follow?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I talk a normal amount, but lately... yeah, it's getting hard for people to follow me. Like, I actually came in because of that. When I'm at the store and I try to explain the inventory numbers or, you know, the weekend schedule to my team, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll be talking about the delivery trucks, and then suddenly I'm talking about Thursdays, and then the church grill, and then my boss's tone of voice. And they just look at me like they have no idea what I'm saying. Which is frustrating because I'm the supervisor, you know? I have to keep things running. Like with the schedule, if we don't have enough coverage on a Saturday, it's a disaster. Especially if there's a big sale. We had this huge clearance event last month, and corporate sent us these massive banners to hang from the ceiling. They were supposed to be easy to put up, but the grommets were all spaced wrong, so we had to use zip ties. Zip ties are amazing, honestly. You can fix almost anything with a zip tie. I keep a whole jar of them in my garage. My neighbor, Tom, he borrowed some to fix his lawnmower handle, and he never brought them back. Not that I need them back, they're cheap, but it's the principle of the thing. You borrow something, you return it. But yeah, people look at me like they can't follow my train of thought when I'm just trying to get the schedule sorted out.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble finding the right words or understanding what words mean?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think I have trouble finding words or understanding them. I know what words mean. Like, I read a lot, or I used to anyway. I used to read those thick paperback thrillers, the ones you get at the airport spinner racks. John Grisham, Tom Clancy. I had a whole bookshelf full of them in my old apartment. That apartment was so small, though, the bookshelf took up half the living room wall. It was one of those cheap particle board ones you put together yourself with an Allen wrench. I hate Allen wrenches. You always strip the screw head or lose the wrench before you're done. I bought a real nice tool set a few years ago, Craftsman, with the lifetime warranty. Kept it in the trunk of my car for emergencies. Good thing, too, because my radiator hose blew out on Interstate 95 right during rush hour. Steam everywhere. I had to pull over onto the shoulder, cars flying past me at seventy miles an hour. It's terrifying being stuck on the side of the highway like that. You just feel the wind from the semis shaking your whole car. But I had the tools to loosen the clamp and wait for a tow. Anyway, no, the words themselves aren't the problem, I know what they mean.\n\nDoctor: Did this change start after a stroke, head injury, seizure, or other brain problem?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't had a stroke or anything like that. No head injuries. I mean, I did hit my head on the kitchen cabinet a couple of weeks ago. I was trying to reach the top shelf where we keep the good coffee mugs. My wife, she bought these really nice ceramic ones from a craft fair up in Vermont. We went there for our anniversary a few years back. It was in October, right when the leaves were changing. The colors were just unbelievable, all these bright reds and oranges. We stayed in this little bed and breakfast that had a wood-burning stove in the lobby. The owner made fresh blueberry muffins every morning. I've tried to make blueberry muffins at home, but they never turn out right. They always get soggy on the bottom. I think it's because I use frozen berries instead of fresh ones. But fresh blueberries are so expensive at the grocery store right now. Everything is expensive, really. The price of gas just went up another ten cents yesterday. I saw it on the sign when I was driving to work. But yeah, no strokes or seizures or anything wrong with my brain like that. Just the cabinet door.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever stutter or have similar speech problems as a child?\n\nPatient: No, I didn't stutter when I was a kid. I talked just fine. Actually, I was in the school play in fifth grade. I played one of the townspeople in this play about the Gold Rush. My mom made my costume out of an old flannel shirt and some corduroy pants. She sewed these little patches on the knees to make it look authentic. She was really good at sewing. She had this old Singer sewing machine, the kind with the foot pedal, not the electric ones. It weighed a ton. It was cast iron, I think. She kept it in the corner of the dining room, right next to this big fern she had. That fern lived forever. I think she watered it with leftover tea or something, she read that in a magazine. Magazines used to have all sorts of weird tips like that. Now you just look everything up on the internet, but back then you had to subscribe to Reader's Digest or Better Homes and Gardens. We used to get a whole stack of them in the mail every month. The mail carrier, Mr. Henderson, he was always complaining about how heavy his bag was. He walked his whole route, no mail truck. He wore those funny little blue shorts in the summer. But yeah, no stuttering or anything when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you had this kind of disorganized, hard-to-follow speech since childhood, or did it begin only in the last few years?\n\nPatient: No, definitely not since I was a kid. It's really just been the last six months or so. It's been pretty noticeable at work, actually, out on the sales floor. I'll be trying to explain a task to one of the associates and my explanations just get all jumbled up, or I'll do these odd stops right in the middle of a sentence. Being on the sales floor all day is tough on you, anyway. We have those polished concrete floors and they just absolutely ruin your knees and your lower back after an eight-hour shift. I had to go to the pharmacy and buy those thick gel inserts for my work boots. I wear the Timberland ones with the composite toe. They're a lot lighter than the steel toe ones. Plus, steel toes get freezing cold in the winter. I remember unloading a delivery truck out back during that big blizzard a few years ago and my toes were practically numb. We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. Last year he put in this massive above-ground pool, but he didn't level the ground right, so the water is like three inches higher on one side. It drives me crazy just looking at it over the fence. I tried to tell him he needed to lay down some sand first, but Gary never listens. He's a retired plumber, so he thinks he knows everything about water. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed any problems with your memory or thinking that are new around the same time as the speech changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have trouble with stuttering or repeated sound blocks when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: No, I never had any stuttering or blocks or anything like that when I was a kid. I always spoke pretty clearly, actually. I even used to do the morning announcements in middle school over the PA system. Speaking of PA systems, the intercom at the store has been acting up for weeks now. You press the button on the register phone to page someone to the paint desk, and all you get is this loud, screeching feedback sound. It just pierces right through your ears. One of our cashiers, Brenda, she accidentally left the page button locked the other day and the whole store had to listen to that awful static for like five minutes until I ran up to the front to fix it. Brenda's a sweet lady, but she is just not great with technology. She actually still uses a flip phone. I tried to explain to her that she could get a smartphone for pretty cheap these days, especially if you buy them refurbished. My son upgraded his a few months ago and gave me his old one, so I know you can get good use out of the older models. You just have to make sure you buy a heavy-duty case for it. I got one of those OtterBox ones. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty understanding other people when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I understand people just fine. I hear what they're saying and I get it. It's just my own mouth that's the issue. But listening? No problem. Like, I listen to the customers all day long. Some of them, though, they don't even know what they want when they come in. A guy came in yesterday looking for a specific type of anchor for drywall, but he kept calling it a \"wall screw.\" I had to ask him like five questions just to figure out he needed a toggle bolt. Drywall is tricky, you know? If you don't hit a stud, whatever you hang is just going to rip right out of the gypsum. My brother-in-law tried to mount a 65-inch TV in his living room without finding the studs first. He just used regular plastic anchors. Middle of the night, crash! The whole thing came down, took a huge chunk of the wall with it, and completely smashed his glass coffee table. It was a nightmare to clean up. Glass was everywhere, even in the carpet fibers. You really need a good shop vac for something like that, not just a regular household vacuum. But anyway, yeah, I understand people perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you think the main problem is that your words come out in the wrong order or jumbled, rather than you repeating sounds or getting stuck on sounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely more jumbled. Not stuck sounds or anything like that. I actually came in because when I try to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to the floor staff, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the delivery trucks to Thursdays to the church grill to my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. It's frustrating because it makes sense in my head. \n\nBut speaking of the church grill, we really need to replace the grates on that thing before the spring picnic. Cast iron is the only way to go, but you have to season it right or it's useless. You rub it down with oil and bake it. If you don't, it rusts so fast. My wife left my favorite cast iron skillet soaking in soapy water in the sink overnight once, and it was a mess. I spent an hour scrubbing it with coarse steel wool the next morning. We sell a ton of steel wool in the paint aisle, actually. Mostly to people refinishing old furniture. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. You really have to wear a good N95 mask, otherwise you'll be blowing brown dust out of your nose for a week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't repeat sounds or get stuck on syllables. Like I was saying, it's just the words coming out in the wrong order. I don't get stuck on the letters themselves. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. That's why I always tell the DIY customers to buy the dust-control kind of compound, even though it costs a few bucks more. It's totally worth it so you don't ruin your HVAC system. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.

We actually lost power at the store for two whole days during that storm. We had to crack open the inventory and use the heavy-duty Maglites just to see what we were doing in the aisles. The batteries drain so fast on those older models, though. D-cell batteries are getting so expensive, too. I always tell customers to buy the rechargeable LED flashlights now because they last way longer and you don't have to keep buying batteries. But yeah, no, it hasn't been a lifelong thing, just about six months with the jumbled talking.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications, or used any alcohol or recreational drugs, around the time this speech problem began?\n\nPatient: No, no new medications or anything. And I don't really drink or do drugs. I mean, I might have a beer if I'm at a barbecue or something, but that's about it. We actually had a barbecue over Labor Day weekend. My neighbor, Gary, he got one of those big offset smokers. He smoked a brisket for, like, fourteen hours. He used hickory wood, which gives it this really strong flavor. I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. I tried to tell him he needed to lay down some sand first, but Gary never listens. He's a retired plumber, so he thinks he knows everything about water. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. Just the usual stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you also noticed any problems with your memory or thinking that are new around the same time as the speech changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed any problems with my memory or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. It smells great when you first cut it, though, like a giant pencil shavings. I used to love sharpening my pencils in school, just to smell the wood. We had those sharpener things mounted on the wall, the ones with the little crank handle. But yeah, no memory problems, just the talking thing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have trouble with stuttering or repeated sound blocks when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: No, I never had any stuttering or blocks or anything like that when I was a kid. I always spoke pretty clearly, actually. I even used to do the morning announcements in middle school over the PA system. Speaking of PA systems, the intercom at the store has been acting up for weeks now. You press the button on the register phone to page someone to the paint desk, and all you get is this loud, screeching feedback sound. It just pierces right through your ears. One of our cashiers, Brenda, she accidentally left the page button locked the other day and the whole store had to listen to that awful static for like five minutes until I ran up to the front to fix it. Brenda's a sweet lady, but she is just not great with technology. She actually still uses a flip phone. I tried to explain to her that she could get a smartphone for pretty cheap these days, especially if you buy them refurbished. My son upgraded his a few months ago and gave me his old one, so I know you can get good use out of the older models. You just have to make sure you buy a heavy-duty case for it. I got one of those OtterBox ones. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty understanding other people when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I understand people just fine. I hear what they're saying and I get it. It's just my own mouth that's the issue. But listening? No problem. Like, I listen to the customers all day long. Some of them, though, they don't even know what they want when they come in. A guy came in yesterday looking for a specific type of anchor for drywall, but he kept calling it a \"wall screw.\" I had to ask him like five questions just to figure out he needed a toggle bolt. Drywall is tricky, you know? If you don't hit a stud, whatever you hang is just going to rip right out of the gypsum. My brother-in-law tried to mount a 65-inch TV in his living room without finding the studs first. He just used regular plastic anchors. Middle of the night, crash! The whole thing came down, took a huge chunk of the wall with it, and completely smashed his glass coffee table. It was a nightmare to clean up. Glass was everywhere, even in the carpet fibers. You really need a good shop vac for something like that, not just a regular household vacuum. But anyway, yeah, I understand people perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you think the main problem is that your words come out in the wrong order or jumbled, rather than you repeating sounds or getting stuck on sounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely more jumbled. Not stuck sounds or anything like that. I actually came in because when I try to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to the floor staff, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the delivery trucks to Thursdays to the church grill to my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. It's frustrating because it makes sense in my head. \n\nBut speaking of the church grill, we really need to replace the grates on that thing before the spring picnic. Cast iron is the only way to go, but you have to season it right or it's useless. You rub it down with oil and bake it. If you don't, it rusts so fast. My wife left my favorite cast iron skillet soaking in soapy water in the sink overnight once, and it was a mess. I spent an hour scrubbing it with coarse steel wool the next morning. We sell a ton of steel wool in the paint aisle, actually. Mostly to people refinishing old furniture. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. You really have to wear a good N95 mask, otherwise you'll be blowing brown dust out of your nose for a week.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you repeat sounds or syllables, like getting stuck on the first part of a word?\n\nPatient: No, I don't repeat sounds or get stuck on syllables. Like I was saying, it's just the words coming out in the wrong order. I don't get stuck on the letters themselves. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. That's why I always tell the DIY customers to buy the dust-control kind of compound, even though it costs a few bucks more. It's totally worth it so you don't ruin your HVAC system. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. No, it doesn't feel like I'm straining or anything. The words flow out just fine, they just... go wherever they want once they're out. It's not like I'm forcing them. Speaking of forcing things, though, we had a guy trying to force a return on a power drill last week that clearly had been dropped in mud. He was swearing up and down he bought it like that. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it "didn't have enough power." We know what they're doing. Pressure washing is satisfying, though. Watching all that grime just blast away from the concrete... it's like erasing years of dirt in seconds. You have to be careful with the nozzle tip, though. If you use the zero-degree red tip on wood siding, you'll splinter it right to pieces. I had to explain that to a customer just yesterday who wanted to clean his deck. But yeah, no strain when I talk.

I prefer applewood, personally. It's a little sweeter, you know? Gary is always buying these crazy expensive gadgets for his backyard. But plumbing is totally different from landscaping. Anyway, no, no new pills or drugs or anything like that. My memory is fine. I can still remember all the SKU numbers for the top-selling items in the hardware department. Like the 16-ounce claw hammer, that's SKU 48291. We sell so many of those, especially in the spring when everyone decides they're going to build a deck or fix their fence. Fences are a lot of work, though. You have to dig the post holes below the frost line, otherwise the ground freezes and heaves the posts right out of the dirt. It happened to my cousin in Minnesota. He built this beautiful cedar fence, spent thousands of dollars on the lumber, and didn't dig deep enough. The very next winter, the whole thing was leaning like the Leaning Tower of Pisa. He was so mad. He tried to fix it with these metal brackets, but it never looked right after that. Cedar is so expensive, too. They cost like fifty bucks, which feels like a rip-off for a piece of plastic and rubber, but if you drop your phone on that polished concrete at work, the screen is going to shatter into a million pieces. So it's worth the investment, I guess. But yeah, no, no stuttering when I was growing up.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any difficulty understanding other people when they talk to you?\n\nPatient: No, I understand people just fine. I hear what they're saying and I get it. It's just my own mouth that's the issue. But listening? No problem. Like, I listen to the customers all day long. Some of them, though, they don't even know what they want when they come in. A guy came in yesterday looking for a specific type of anchor for drywall, but he kept calling it a \"wall screw.\" I had to ask him like five questions just to figure out he needed a toggle bolt. Drywall is tricky, you know? If you don't hit a stud, whatever you hang is just going to rip right out of the gypsum. My brother-in-law tried to mount a 65-inch TV in his living room without finding the studs first. He just used regular plastic anchors. Middle of the night, crash! The whole thing came down, took a huge chunk of the wall with it, and completely smashed his glass coffee table. It was a nightmare to clean up. Glass was everywhere, even in the carpet fibers. You really need a good shop vac for something like that, not just a regular household vacuum. But anyway, yeah, I understand people perfectly fine.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you think the main problem is that your words come out in the wrong order or jumbled, rather than you repeating sounds or getting stuck on sounds?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's definitely more jumbled. Not stuck sounds or anything like that. I actually came in because when I try to explain inventory numbers or the weekend schedule to the floor staff, my words just start jumping all over the place. I'll go from talking about the delivery trucks to Thursdays to the church grill to my boss’s tone of voice, and people just look at me like they can’t follow what I’m saying. It's frustrating because it makes sense in my head. \n\nBut speaking of the church grill, we really need to replace the grates on that thing before the spring picnic. Cast iron is the only way to go, but you have to season it right or it's useless. You rub it down with oil and bake it. If you don't, it rusts so fast. My wife left my favorite cast iron skillet soaking in soapy water in the sink overnight once, and it was a mess. I spent an hour scrubbing it with coarse steel wool the next morning. We sell a ton of steel wool in the paint aisle, actually. Mostly to people refinishing old furniture. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. You really have to wear a good N95 mask, otherwise you'll be blowing brown dust out of your nose for a week.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you repeat sounds or syllables, like getting stuck on the first part of a word?\n\nPatient: No, I don't repeat sounds or get stuck on syllables. Like I was saying, it's just the words coming out in the wrong order. I don't get stuck on the letters themselves. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. That's why I always tell the DIY customers to buy the dust-control kind of compound, even though it costs a few bucks more. It's totally worth it so you don't ruin your HVAC system. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to speak, do you feel like the words come out with effort or strain?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like I'm straining or anything. The words flow out just fine, they just... go wherever they want once they're out. It's not like I'm forcing them. Speaking of forcing things, though, we had a guy trying to force a return on a power drill last week that clearly had been dropped in mud. He was swearing up and down he bought it like that. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it \"didn't have enough power.\" We know what they're doing. Pressure washing is satisfying, though. Watching all that grime just blast away from the concrete... it's like erasing years of dirt in seconds. You have to be careful with the nozzle tip, though. If you use the zero-degree red tip on wood siding, you'll splinter it right to pieces. I had to explain that to a customer just yesterday who wanted to clean his deck. My wife left my favorite cast iron skillet soaking in soapy water in the sink overnight once, and it was a mess. I spent an hour scrubbing it with coarse steel wool the next morning. We sell a ton of steel wool in the paint aisle, actually. Mostly to people refinishing old furniture. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. That's why I always tell the DIY customers to buy the dust-control kind of compound, even though it costs a few bucks more. It's totally worth it so you don't ruin your HVAC system. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to speak, do you feel like the words come out with effort or strain?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like I'm straining or anything. The words flow out just fine, they just... go wherever they want once they're out. It's not like I'm forcing them. Speaking of forcing things, though, we had a guy trying to force a return on a power drill last week that clearly had been dropped in mud. He was swearing up and down he bought it like that. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it \"didn't have enough power.\" We know what they're doing. Pressure washing is satisfying, though. Watching all that grime just blast away from the concrete... it's like erasing years of dirt in seconds. You have to be careful with the nozzle tip, though. Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. You really have to wear a good N95 mask, otherwise you'll be blowing brown dust out of your nose for a week.\n\nDoctor: When you speak, do you repeat sounds or syllables, like getting stuck on the first part of a word?\n\nPatient: No, I don't repeat sounds or get stuck on syllables. Like I was saying, it's just the words coming out in the wrong order. I don't get stuck on the letters themselves. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. That's why I always tell the DIY customers to buy the dust-control kind of compound, even though it costs a few bucks more. It's totally worth it so you don't ruin your HVAC system. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to speak, do you feel like the words come out with effort or strain?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like I'm straining or anything. The words flow out just fine, they just... go wherever they want once they're out. It's not like I'm forcing them. Speaking of forcing things, though, we had a guy trying to force a return on a power drill last week that clearly had been dropped in mud. He was swearing up and down he bought it like that. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it \"didn't have enough power.\" We know what they're doing. Pressure washing is satisfying, though. Watching all that grime just blast away from the concrete... it's like erasing years of dirt in seconds. You have to be careful with the nozzle tip, though. If you use the zero-degree red tip on wood siding, you'll splinter it right to pieces. I had to explain that to a customer just yesterday who wanted to clean his deck. But yeah, no strain when I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other new neurological symptoms around the same time, like weakness, numbness, trouble walking, or vision changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. No weakness or numbness, and I can walk and see just fine. Honestly, the only walking trouble I have is when I've been standing on that concrete floor at the store for an eight-hour shift. Concrete is just brutal on the joints. That's why I always tell the new hires to invest in good insoles right away. You don't want to wait until your knees are screaming at you. I bought this pair of gel inserts last month, and they actually make a huge difference. We sell them right by the registers, actually, kind of an impulse buy item. It's funny what people will grab while they're waiting in line. Batteries, candy bars, super glue. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. Speaking of forcing things, though, we had a guy trying to force a return on a power drill last week that clearly had been dropped in mud. He was swearing up and down he bought it like that. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it \"didn't have enough power.\" We know what they're doing. Pressure washing is satisfying, though. Watching all that grime just blast away from the concrete... it's like erasing years of dirt in seconds. You have to be careful with the nozzle tip, though. If you use the zero-degree red tip on wood siding, you'll splinter it right to pieces. I had to explain that to a customer just yesterday who wanted to clean his deck. But yeah, no strain when I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other new neurological symptoms around the same time, like weakness, numbness, trouble walking, or vision changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. No weakness or numbness, and I can walk and see just fine. Honestly, the only walking trouble I have is when I've been standing on that concrete floor at the store for an eight-hour shift. Concrete is just brutal on the joints. That's why I always tell the new hires to invest in good insoles right away. You don't want to wait until your knees are screaming at you. No, I never really had any trouble with that. I got jokes and sarcasm just fine when I was a kid, my whole family was pretty sarcastic actually. Speaking of jokes, though, we sell these little novelty signs at the hardware store, like the ones that say "Trespassers will be composted" or "Beware of Wife." Refinishing furniture takes a lot of patience, sanding everything down by hand to get all the old varnish off. The dust gets absolutely everywhere, though. Like I was saying, it's just the words coming out in the wrong order. I don't get stuck on the letters themselves. Speaking of getting stuck, though, we had a pallet jack get completely stuck in the receiving bay yesterday. One of the wheels just locked up entirely on a stray piece of a zip tie. You can't just drag a loaded pallet of joint compound when a wheel is seized, it's way too heavy. Joint compound comes in those big five-gallon buckets, and they weigh an absolute ton. People buy them for skim coating walls, which is really an art form if you think about it. You need a really wide taping knife, like a 12-inch or 14-inch one, and a very steady hand. Otherwise, you end up with ridges everywhere and you're sanding for days. Sanding drywall is just the absolute worst, the dust gets into everything, your hair, your clothes, your lungs. But yeah, no, my words don't get stuck like that.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to speak, do you feel like the words come out with effort or strain?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn't feel like I'm straining or anything. I mean, who buys a mud-caked drill? You can't just return something you ruined. Our return policy is pretty generous, honestly, 90 days with a receipt. But people still try to game the system. They'll buy a pressure washer on a Friday, clean their whole driveway, and try to bring it back on Monday saying it \"didn't have enough power.\" We know what they're doing. If you use the zero-degree red tip on wood siding, you'll splinter it right to pieces. I had to explain that to a customer just yesterday who wanted to clean his deck. But yeah, no strain when I talk.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other new neurological symptoms around the same time, like weakness, numbness, trouble walking, or vision changes?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that. It's funny what people will grab while they're waiting in line. Batteries, candy bars, super glue. Super glue is one of those things you never think you need until something breaks and you're scrambling for it. I tried to super glue a coffee mug back together once, the one my daughter got me for Father's Day. It held up for about a week before the hot coffee just melted the adhesive right off and it spilled all over my lap. Burned like crazy. But yeah, no new neurological stuff, just the jumbled words.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you have trouble understanding jokes, sarcasm, or other people’s facial expressions and tone of voice?\n\nPatient: No, I never really had any trouble with that.
